Imiquimod 5% (Aldara)TLR-7 agonist — activates innate immunityOff-label oral use; Phase I/II at USC; the prof’s Case 2 agent
5-ALA Photodynamic TherapyPhotosensitizer + visible light → ROS-mediated cell deathSelectively kills dysplastic cells; tissue-preserving; 66–100% CR; 8.3% MT at 3 yr
Diclofenac topicalNSAID — COX inhibitionUsed in actinic cheilitis
Vitamin D3Differentiation, immune modulationAdjunctive; deficiency widely associated with cancer

Part 3 — Emerging Therapies & Clinical Trials

The cutting edge of non-surgical dysplasia management — BWH/DFCI pipeline
CategoryAgentMechanismEvidenceStatus
Natural Compounds Curcumin 3.6 g/day NF-κB inhibition; antioxidant RCT: 67.5% response vs. 55.3% placebo (p=0.03) No histologic difference; oral bioavailability poor
Oncolytic Viruses ONYX-015 (mouthwash) p53-selective adenovirus 37% histologic resolution; mostly transient Investigational
rAd-p53 / Gendicine (injection) p53 gene transfer Phase I: 72% clinical response; safe and feasible China only; investigational
RP2 (intralesional injection) Modified HSV-1 oncolytic virus Phase II Clinical Trial at BWH/DFCI Active ongoing trial
TLR9 Agonists Vidutolimod (± ICB) VLP activates pDCs via TLR9 ↑ IFN-γ, IL-12, TNF-α; ↓ Ki-67 in lesions Investigational
Emerging APG-157 (zinflavonoid mixture) Flavonoid multi-target Phase IIa: some severe dysplasia → no dysplasia Ongoing
Immune checkpoint inhibitors (PD-1/PD-L1) PD-1/PD-L1 blockade Being evaluated for dysplasia prevention Investigational

Active Clinical Trials to Know: (1) RP2 (oncolytic modified HSV-1) at BWH/DFCI — Phase II, intralesional injection. (2) Imiquimod 5% at USC — Phase I/II. These represent the cutting edge of non-surgical dysplasia management and were specifically flagged by the prof.

Imiquimod mechanism in plain English: imiquimod binds toll-like receptor 7 (TLR-7) on local dendritic cells and macrophages. TLR-7 normally senses single-stranded viral RNA — binding it triggers a strong type-I interferon response (IFN-α/β), recruits CD8+ T cells, activates NK cells. Dysplastic keratinocytes that escape immune surveillance get re-recognized and destroyed. Side effect: aggressive local inflammation (redness, soreness, ulceration) for the first 2–4 weeks — that is the drug working.

ALA-PDT mechanism in plain English: 5-aminolevulinic acid is a precursor in the heme synthesis pathway. Apply it topically and dysplastic cells preferentially absorb & convert it into protoporphyrin IX (because their metabolism is dysregulated). Then shine red/blue visible light: protoporphyrin IX absorbs the photon, transfers energy to molecular oxygen, generates reactive oxygen species (singlet oxygen) that destroy the dysplastic cell from inside. Selectivity comes from differential ALA uptake/conversion.

  • Imiquimod = TLR-7 agonist → innate immune activation; Phase I/II at USC
  • ALA-PDT = ALA → protoporphyrin IX → light → ROS → cell death; 66–100% CR
  • Beta-carotene may INCREASE risk in smokers (CARET trial); RR 0.71 NS
  • 13-cis-RA: 67% CR; Vitamin A 300k IU: 52% CR; Fenretinide: 87% CR
  • Bleomycin 0.5–1%: 25–40% CR
  • Curcumin 3.6 g/d: RCT 67.5% vs 55.3% placebo (p=0.03); no histo difference
  • RP2 (modified HSV-1): Phase II at BWH/DFCI (intralesional)
  • ONYX-015 mouthwash: 37% histologic resolution; rAd-p53: 72% clinical response (China)
  • Vidutolimod (TLR9 VLP): ↑IFN-γ, IL-12, TNF-α; ↓Ki-67
  • APG-157: Phase IIa, some severe → no dysplasia
  • Calcipotriene ± 5-FU = NCCN preferred for cutaneous field cancerization
09

Other OPMDs: OSF, OLP, OLL

Submucous fibrosis, lichen planus, lichenoid lesions — and how to tell them apart

Oral Submucous Fibrosis (OSF)

The areca-nut / betel-quid disease — 5.1% MT, 36 mo to cancer

OSF malignant transformation rate: ~5.1%. Median time from OSF onset to oral cancer: ~36 months. Cause is areca nut (the active ingredient in betel quid) — a Group 1 IARC carcinogen. Prevalent in South & Southeast Asian populations.

Clinical features

  • Burning sensation with spicy foods (early symptom)
  • Progressive blanching of mucosa → marble-like whitening
  • Palpable fibrous bands on buccal mucosa, soft palate, lips
  • Late: trismus (limited mouth opening) — from submucosal fibrosis around muscles of mastication
  • Tongue depapillation, atrophic mucosa
  • Condition is considered irreversible once established

OSF mechanism — how a chewed nut causes fibrosis & cancer: areca nut releases arecoline and copper-rich extracts that drive submucosal fibroblasts into a profibrotic phenotype. Fibroblasts upregulate collagen type I/III and TGF-β, depositing dense fibrous bands that stiffen the mucosa (hence trismus). Simultaneously, chronic injury induces epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition (EMT): surface keratinocytes lose E-cadherin, gain mesenchymal markers, and the field becomes primed for malignant transformation.

HIF / EMT pathway in OSF: the same dense fibrosis that causes trismus also chokes off oxygen delivery to the overlying epithelium. Hypoxic stress activates HIF-1α, which drives SNAIL/SLUG transcription factors that suppress E-cadherin and trigger EMT. Cells shed their epithelial polarity, become motile, accumulate genetic damage. This is the molecular bridge from OSF to invasive SCC.

Oral Lichen Planus (OLP)

T-cell–mediated autoimmune disease, idiopathic — bilateral, symmetric

OLP is bilateral, symmetric, idiopathic. Sites: buccal mucosa > tongue > gingiva. Classic finding: Wickham striae — lacy white reticular network. Six clinical subtypes: reticular, papular, plaque-like, atrophic, erosive, bullous. ~1.4% MT rate (controversial — risk concentrates in atrophic/erosive forms). The controversy: some experts argue that reported “OLP transformation” cases were actually misdiagnosed oral lichenoid dysplasia from the beginning, not true lichen planus. Regardless, patients with OLP — especially erosive/atrophic forms — should be monitored long-term.

Histology (4 classic features)

  • Hyperkeratosis (parakeratosis) of surface
  • Saw-tooth rete ridges
  • Band-like (lichenoid) lymphocytic infiltrate hugging the basement membrane
  • Liquefactive degeneration of the basal cell layer with apoptotic keratinocytes (Civatte bodies)

Treatment

  • First-line: topical corticosteroids (clobetasol gel, dexamethasone elixir swish & spit, fluocinonide ointment)
  • Severe / refractory: topical tacrolimus / pimecrolimus, intralesional steroid, systemic immunomodulators
  • Eliminate amalgam / contact triggers if a lichenoid pattern is suspected

Oral Lichenoid Lesions (OLL)

“Looks like OLP, but caused by something specific” — three types

OLL malignant transformation: ~1.4% — the same as OLP per the prof’s slide. Don’t undertreat OLL just because it has an identifiable trigger. The prof’s specific instruction: “ask the pathologist about architectural dysplasia” — OLL can mask underlying dysplastic change. Oral lichenoid dysplasia (one of the three OLL types) has a higher MT than true OLP and requires close surveillance.

Three Types of OLL:

  • Oral lichenoid drug reactions — NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, beta-blockers, antimalarials, hydroxychloroquine, hepatitis-C antivirals, thiazide diuretics
  • Oral lichenoid contact reactions — amalgam, composite, gold, cinnamon, mint flavorings (often unilateral, adjacent to restoration)
  • Oral lichenoid dysplasia — dysplastic lesion with lichen planus-like histologic features; higher MT potential than true OLP; requires biopsy and close follow-up

GVHD (graft-versus-host disease after allogeneic HCT) and Hepatitis C association can also produce lichenoid lesions — ask about HCT and HCV history in patients with atypical OLP presentation.

OLP vs OLL — how to actually tell them apart

FeatureOLPOLL
DistributionBilateral, symmetricOften unilateral, adjacent to a contact trigger or drug-related
EtiologyIdiopathic (autoimmune T-cell mediated)Identifiable (drug, contact, GVHD, HCV)
HistologyClassic band-like lymphocytic infiltrate at basement membrane; saw-tooth rete; Civatte bodiesSimilar but often deeper, more diffuse infiltrate; eosinophils may be present
Reaction to triggersNone — no identifiable agent; resolves only with immunosuppressionResolves or improves when trigger removed
MT rate~1.4%~1.4% (per prof’s slide); but oral lichenoid dysplasia subtype is higher
TreatmentTopical steroids, tacrolimusRemove trigger first; then topical steroids if persists

“Lichenoid contact reaction” vs OLP: if you see a white striated patch on the buccal mucosa that only contacts a specific amalgam or crown, and the contralateral side is normal, that’s a contact lichenoid — remove the restoration and it resolves. True OLP is bilateral and doesn’t care what restorations are present. The give-away is asymmetry.

Erythroplakia ~90% already dysplasia/CIS/SCC at biopsy — do not “watch and wait.” Even a small red patch > 2 weeks needs immediate biopsy. Test-question framing: “which OPMD has the highest rate of severe histology at first biopsy?” Answer: erythroplakia. Median time to cancer = 3.7 months.

  • OSF: 5.1% MT, 36 mo to cancer, areca nut, irreversible
  • OSF mechanism: arecoline → fibroblast EMT → HIF-1α → SNAIL/SLUG → carcinoma
  • OLP: bilateral, ~1.4% MT, Wickham striae; saw-tooth rete + band lymph infiltrate + Civatte bodies
  • OLL 3 types: drug reactions (NSAIDs/ACE-I/BB/antimalarials) + contact reactions (amalgam/cinnamon/mint) + oral lichenoid dysplasia (higher MT)
  • OLP controversy: “transformation” cases may have been misdiagnosed oral lichenoid dysplasia all along
10

Squamous Cell Carcinoma

The main act — epidemiology, risk factors, clinical presentation, warning signs, referral workup

Image: TBD — SCC lateral tongue clinical presentation

SCC is >90% of all oral malignancies. 7th most common cancer worldwide. Median age at diagnosis ~66. M:F ratio 2–4:1 (male predominance shrinking as female smoking + HPV exposure rise). Rising incidence especially in younger patients without traditional risk factors.

>90%of all oral malignancies
7thmost common cancer worldwide
~66median age at diagnosis
2–4 : 1M : F ratio (narrowing)

Risk factors — the prof’s full list

CategorySpecific factorNotes
TobaccoCigarettes / cigars / pipesDose-dependent; pack-years
Smokeless (chewing, dip, snuff)Risk localizes to where the quid sits
Betel quid / areca nutGroup 1 carcinogen; OSF + SCC; 600M+ users worldwide
AlcoholHeavy chronic useSolvent + acetaldehyde mutagen
ComboTobacco + alcohol~30× multiplicative
GeneticFanconi anemiaOR ~40.6 — highest single OR
Dyskeratosis congenitaTelomere maintenance defect; ~1,000× baseline
ViralHPV-16 (oropharynx only, NOT oral cavity)See section 12
LightUV (lip vermilion only)NOT intraoral SCC
IatrogenicChronic immunosuppression (transplant, HIV)Higher SCC incidence
Oral hygienePoor OH, chronic mucosal traumaCo-factor, not independent driver
DietLow fruit/vegetable intake; iron deficiency (Plummer-Vinson)Modest contribution

Field cancerization — why SCC patients keep getting new SCCs: the entire upper aerodigestive epithelium has been bathed in the same carcinogens (smoke + ethanol). By the time one tumor is clinically visible, thousands of other dispersed clones across the mucosa have already accumulated TP53 / NOTCH1 / FAT1 / CDKN2A mutations. Slaughter coined “field cancerization” in 1953. Clinically: lifetime risk of a second primary SCC is ~3–5% per year — which is why post-treatment patients need lifelong oral exam screening.

Sites & presentation

Lateral / ventral tongue
42% — #1 site
Floor of mouth
16%
Other intraoral
42%

Where intraoral SCC actually shows up: lateral & ventral tongue (42%) > floor of mouth (16%) > everything else (gingiva, buccal, palate, retromolar trigone). The lateral tongue is the #1 site overall at 41.7% of all oral cavity cancers. Rising incidence in younger patients often without traditional risk factors.

Clinical Presentation — Warning Signs

Any of these should prompt biopsy or urgent referral
  • Non-healing ulcer persisting >2–3 weeks after removing obvious irritants (sharp tooth, denture flange)
  • Exophytic mass or indurated area — induration on palpation is the most reliable physical finding
  • Persistent red or white patch that doesn’t resolve
  • Unexplained bleeding, numbness, or pain
  • Fixation to underlying structures
  • Firm, fixed, non-tender cervical lymphadenopathy
  • Rolled or raised borders
  • Nonhealing socket after extraction (occult tumor in the bone)
  • Loose teeth in non-perio patient (bony invasion)
  • Numbness of lip / tongue / cheek (perineural invasion)

~50% of oral SCCs at diagnosis are NON-ulcerated. Don’t anchor on “oral cancer = ulcer.” A persistent, indurated, asymmetric red or red/white plaque can be SCC without any ulceration. Induration on palpation is the most reliable physical finding.

Referral & Workup

Your role as a dentist: early detection and prompt referral
  • Urgent referral to oral and maxillofacial surgery or head & neck oncology
  • Incisional biopsy for histopathologic confirmation (do not delay out of seeding fear)
  • CT with contrast and/or MRI for locoregional staging
  • PET-CT for distant metastasis evaluation
  • Multidisciplinary tumor board (surgery, radiation oncology, medical oncology)

Perform systematic oral cancer screening at every visit. Any suspicious lesion persisting >2–3 weeks after removal of irritants warrants biopsy or referral. Do not adopt a “watch and wait” approach for clinically suspicious lesions. With erythroplakia, median time to cancer is only 3.7 months.

Lip vermilion SCC is different. Lower-lip SCC behaves like cutaneous SCC: UV-driven, much better prognosis (5-yr survival >90%), low metastatic potential. Don’t conflate it with intraoral SCC even though both are “oral.”

11

AJCC 8th Edition TNM

The staging table — what changed in the 8th edition and why

AJCC 8th edition (2018) made two big changes for oral cavity SCC:

  1. Depth of invasion (DOI) was added to T-staging because tumor depth predicts mortality independently of surface size.
  2. Extranodal extension (ENE) was incorporated into N-staging because ENE+ disease has dramatically worse outcomes.

T — Primary tumor (8th ed)

T stageGreatest dimensionDepth of invasion (DOI)
T1≤ 2 cmDOI ≤ 5 mm
T2≤ 2 cm with DOI > 5–10 mm OR > 2–4 cm with DOI ≤ 10 mmDOI ≤ 10 mm
T3> 4 cm OR any size with DOI > 10 mmDOI > 10 mm
T4aModerately advanced — invades cortical bone of mandible/maxilla, inferior alveolar nerve, FOM, skin of face—
T4bVery advanced — invades masticator space, pterygoid plates, skull base, encases internal carotid artery—

What “depth of invasion” actually measures: DOI is measured by the pathologist from the level of the adjacent normal mucosal basement membrane down to the deepest point of tumor invasion — not from the ulcerated surface. Two tumors of the same surface diameter can have wildly different DOI: a flat ulcerated lesion that has burrowed 8 mm vs an exophytic 2-cm mass with only 3-mm DOI — the first is more dangerous because depth predicts nodal mets and vascular invasion better than surface size.

N — Regional nodes (8th ed) with ENE

N stageDefinition
N0No regional lymph node metastasis
N1Single ipsilateral node, ≤ 3 cm, ENE−
N2aSingle ipsilateral > 3–6 cm, ENE− OR single ipsilateral ≤ 3 cm with ENE+
N2bMultiple ipsilateral nodes, all ≤ 6 cm, ENE−
N2cBilateral or contralateral nodes, ≤ 6 cm, ENE−
N3aAny node > 6 cm, ENE−
N3bAny clinically/pathologically ENE+ disease that doesn’t fit N2a — the worst nodal category

What “extranodal extension (ENE)” means: a metastatic tumor cell deposit inside a lymph node has eaten through the node’s fibrous capsule and is now growing in the surrounding fat or soft tissue. ENE+ disease has ~2× the recurrence rate and substantially worse survival than ENE− disease at the same nodal size. ENE+ patients get adjuvant chemoradiation rather than radiation alone.

Stage groupings & survival

StageTNM5-yr OS
IT1 N0 M0~85%
IIT2 N0 M0~70%
IIIT3 N0 or T1–3 N1~55%
IVAT4a N0–1 or T1–4a N2~35%
IVBAny T4b or any N3~20%
IVCAny M1 (distant mets)~10–15%
  • T1 = ≤2 cm AND DOI ≤5 mm
  • T2 = up to 4 cm AND DOI ≤10 mm
  • T3 = >4 cm OR DOI >10 mm
  • T4a = mod-advanced (cortical bone, IAN, FOM, skin of face)
  • T4b = very advanced (skull base, ICA encasement, pterygoid plates, masticator space)
  • N3b = ENE+ — worst nodal stage; gets adjuvant CRT
  • Stage I 85% → IVC ~10–15% 5-yr OS
  • ENE+ → ~2× recurrence rate
12

HPV-Mediated SCC

Why oropharynx ≠ oral cavity, and why Gardasil 9 matters

Image: TBD — HPV+ oropharyngeal SCC vs HPV- oral SCC

The single most important distinction in this section: HPV-positive SCC of the oropharynx (tonsils, base of tongue, soft palate, posterior pharyngeal wall) is a different disease from HPV-positive SCC of the oral cavity (anterior 2/3 of tongue, FOM, buccal, gingiva, hard palate). Oropharyngeal HPV+ has better prognosis; oral cavity HPV+ does NOT.

HPV+ OPSCC vs HPV− OPSCC vs HPV+ OCSCC

FeatureHPV+ OPSCCHPV− OPSCCHPV+ OCSCC (oral cavity)
Typical patientYounger (50s), white male, non- or light smoker, multiple oral-sex partnersOlder (60s+), heavy smoker/drinkerSame demographic as HPV− OCSCC
SiteTonsil, base of tongue (lymphoid-rich crypts)Tonsil, BOT, soft palateAnterior 2/3 tongue, FOM, etc.
HPV typeHPV-16 (~90%)HPV negativeHPV-16 if any (uncommon)
p16 IHCStrongly + (surrogate)NegativeMay be + or −; not a reliable surrogate in oral cavity
5-yr OS~80–85%~40%Same as HPV− OCSCC (no benefit)
Stage at diagnosisOften N+ early but small TOften advanced TStage-dependent
Treatment responseExcellent — de-escalation trials ongoingStandard CRTStandard surgery ± CRT

HPV E6/E7 oncoproteins — the molecular punch: high-risk HPV types (16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58) integrate into the host genome and the early genes E6 and E7 are persistently expressed. E6 binds and degrades p53 (the genome guardian), and E7 binds and inactivates Rb (the cell-cycle brake). Loss of p53 → no apoptosis after DNA damage. Loss of Rb → constitutive S-phase entry. Result: a cell that divides relentlessly and tolerates mutations.

p16 IHC as an HPV surrogate: when E7 inactivates Rb, the cell loses negative feedback on the CDKN2A locus and p16 protein piles up in the nucleus & cytoplasm. Strong, diffuse p16 positivity (≥ 70% nuclear+cytoplasmic staining) in an OPSCC is the standard surrogate for HPV-driven disease — concordance with HPV PCR > 95%. In oral cavity SCC, p16 is NOT a reliable surrogate — you can be p16+ without HPV driving the tumor.

HPV positivity does NOT confer survival benefit in oral cavity SCC. The good-prognosis HPV+ story is oropharynx-specific. Oral cavity HPV+ tumors do not behave the same way and should not be de-escalated. HPV− OPSCC 5-yr OS is ~40% — not 50–55%.

Gardasil 9 covers HPV types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58. ACIP recommends routine vaccination for all children at age 11–12 (can start at 9), catch-up through age 26, and shared decision-making to age 45. Best efficacy when given before sexual debut. Vaccination is the single most powerful preventive intervention against HPV-driven OPSCC.

  • Gardasil 9 covers: 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58
  • HPV-16 drives ~90% of HPV+ OPSCC
  • p16 IHC = HPV surrogate in OROPHARYNX only
  • HPV+ OPSCC 5-yr OS ~80–85% vs HPV− OPSCC ~40%
  • HPV+ OCSCC: NO survival benefit
  • E6 → degrades p53; E7 → inactivates Rb; p16 piles up (Rb released)
13

Verrucous Carcinoma

The “low-grade SCC” that virtually never metastasizes

Image: TBD — Verrucous carcinoma clinical photo

Verrucous carcinoma is a low-grade variant of SCC. Slow-growing, exophytic, warty/cauliflower-like, locally destructive but virtually NEVER metastasizes (when pure verrucous — mixed verrucous-conventional SCC can). Classic association: chronic smokeless tobacco use at the site where the quid sits.

Clinical features

  • Slow-growing, exophytic, warty (verrucous), white-pink mass
  • Common sites: buccal mucosa, mandibular alveolar ridge, tongue, FOM — especially under chewing-tobacco placement
  • Wide pushing border on histology — not infiltrative
  • Bland cytology — minimal atypia in cells, easy to miss on superficial biopsy
  • Cervical lymphadenopathy is rare and usually reactive (not malignant)

Verrucous carcinoma biology in plain English: verrucous carcinoma cells are well-differentiated keratinocytes — they look almost normal under the microscope, with minimal nuclear atypia. What makes them malignant is architectural, not cytologic: they form thick keratinized rete pegs that push down into the lamina propria as a broad, blunt front, instead of infiltrating as small invasive nests. They lack the molecular machinery for distant metastasis (low MMP expression, intact E-cadherin, low EMT) so they spread by local extension only. They can still destroy bone and soft tissue locally if untreated.

Superficial biopsy → you cannot diagnose verrucous carcinoma. A surface bite captures only the warty top architecture, which looks like benign hyperkeratosis or “verrucous hyperplasia.” The diagnosis depends on seeing the deep pushing border. The biopsy must include the deep submucosa — either an excisional biopsy or a deep incisional bite that goes through the lesion to underlying connective tissue.

Treatment

  • Wide local surgical excision with clear margins — treatment of choice
  • Radiation therapy historically discouraged because of (mostly disproven) reports of anaplastic transformation after RT — modern data suggests RT is acceptable when surgery is not feasible
  • Cervical lymph node dissection is generally NOT indicated in pure verrucous carcinoma (no nodal mets) — reserved for cases with proven mixed conventional SCC component
  • Excellent prognosis: 5-yr OS > 85% with adequate excision; recurrence rate ~10% if margins clear

“Mixed” verrucous + conventional SCC is more dangerous than either pure form. When a pathologist reports a tumor with verrucous architecture but also has foci of conventional SCC, treat it as conventional SCC — node dissection considered, RT may be added. Pure verrucous behavior only applies when all the tumor is verrucous.

  • Verrucous CA: low-grade SCC variant, NO mets in pure form
  • Cause: chronic smokeless tobacco (where quid sits)
  • Diagnosis requires DEEP biopsy (deep pushing border; surface bite = no diagnosis)
  • Treatment: wide local excision; node dissection NOT routine in pure form
  • 5-yr OS > 85%
  • Mixed verrucous + conventional → treat as conventional SCC
14

Oral Melanoma

Rare, deadly, on the palate — and UV is NOT the cause

Image: TBD — Oral melanoma palatal lesion

Oral melanoma is rare (~0.5% of all melanomas, < 1% of oral malignancies) but devastating. ~50% present on the hard palate, with maxillary gingiva second. UV light is NOT the etiology — it cannot reach intraoral mucosa. Cause is unknown for most cases.

Clinical features

  • Asymmetric, irregular pigmented (brown, blue, black) macule or nodule
  • ~15% are amelanotic (pink/red without pigment, worse prognosis) — easy to miss
  • Hard palate > maxillary gingiva > mandibular gingiva > tongue > buccal
  • Most asymptomatic at presentation; ulceration / bleeding is late
  • Often diagnosed at advanced stage — high mortality

Oral mucosal vs cutaneous melanoma

FeatureOral mucosal melanomaCutaneous melanoma
EtiologyUnknown — UV NOT involvedUV exposure
SiteHard palate (~50%), max gingivaSun-exposed skin
Driver mutationsc-KIT (~20–30%), NRAS; BRAF rareBRAF V600E (~50%), NRAS
Mutation burdenLowHigh (UV signature)
Amelanotic rate~15% (pink/red; worse prognosis)Rare; ~2–8%
5-yr OS~15–25% — among the worst of any oral malignancy~92% (all stages combined)
Stage at dxOften advancedUsually localized (visible on skin)
TreatmentWide excision ± neck dissection; immuno (PD-1) for advanced; BRAF inhibitors generally not effectiveExcision + sentinel node; BRAF/MEK inhibitors if BRAF+; PD-1 inhibitors

c-KIT vs BRAF — why the same drug doesn’t work in both: cutaneous melanoma’s UV-driven mutations preferentially hit BRAF (V600E), making it responsive to BRAF inhibitors (vemurafenib, dabrafenib) ± MEK inhibitors. Oral mucosal melanoma rarely has BRAF mutations — instead a meaningful fraction (20–30%) carries activating mutations in c-KIT. c-KIT-mutant oral melanomas may respond to imatinib (the same drug used for GIST and CML) in select cases, but most oral melanomas don’t have a targetable driver, and outcomes lag far behind cutaneous melanoma.

Why oral melanoma prognosis is so bad: three reasons stack up. (1) Late detection — intraoral pigmented lesions are often only noticed at routine dental exam; patients never see them. (2) Vascular-rich submucosa — the palate and gingiva have abundant blood and lymphatic supply, so melanoma cells reach the bloodstream early. (3) Few targetable drivers — without BRAF mutations, the modern targeted-therapy revolution has largely bypassed oral melanoma.

UV is NOT the cause of oral mucosal melanoma. Repeat: not a cause. Mucosal melanoma is a biologically distinct disease from cutaneous melanoma. If a question lists “etiology of oral melanoma” with UV as an option, it’s the wrong answer. Also: amelanotic oral melanoma is ~15% of cases (NOT 25%) — the pink/red lesions that are easy to miss.

Pigmented lesions on the hard palate need biopsy. The differential includes amalgam tattoo (most common), racial pigmentation (bilateral, symmetric, usually gingival), oral melanotic macule (small, < 5 mm, well-defined), Peutz-Jeghers, Addison’s, smoker’s melanosis, and oral melanoma. Asymmetric, irregular, > 5 mm, growing, or located on hard palate / maxillary gingiva — biopsy.

  • Oral melanoma: ~50% hard palate — #1 site
  • UV is NOT the cause
  • c-KIT mutations 20–30%; BRAF rare (opposite of cutaneous)
  • 5-yr OS ~15–25% — worst of any oral malignancy
  • ~15% are amelanotic (pink/red, no pigment) — worse prognosis
  • c-KIT+ → imatinib; PD-1 inhibitors for advanced disease
MASTER STUDY GUIDE — Fully merged from two source files · Built for D3 OD 530 Midterm cram · Sources: 69-slide professor deck + supplementary evidence · Highlighted material flagged · QA-verified: amelanotic melanoma ~15%, erythroplakia ~50% MT (split from 75–90%), HPV− OPSCC ~40% OS
Part 4 of 5

Salivary Gland Disorders

Hyposalivation, xerostomia, Sjögren syndrome, sialolithiasis, sialadenitis, and mucocele — from physiology to chairside management. Dr. Laurel Henderson, DDS, MS · BU GSDM · 02/20/2026

Midterm Exam: 02/27/2026 at 4 PM · 50 questions (MCQ + short answer) · 90 minutes · Option to bring a 5″ × 8″ double-sided handwritten notecard (must turn in at end).
Section 01

Why Saliva Matters

Saliva does far more than keep your mouth wet. It is a multi-functional fluid that protects teeth, fights infection, aids digestion, and even serves as a diagnostic tool.

Image: TBD — Salivary gland anatomy overview with labeled glands
Before we start

The 10 Functions of Saliva

When saliva production drops, all of these break down. This is why hyposalivation is a medical emergency for your teeth.

Think of saliva as your mouth's Swiss Army knife. It doesn't just do one thing — it does ten. When a patient loses salivary function (from medications, radiation, or autoimmune disease), the cascade of problems hits every single one of these domains simultaneously. That is why a "dry mouth" is never just uncomfortable — it is clinically dangerous.

Dental Protection

  • Remineralization — saliva delivers calcium, phosphate, and fluoride back into enamel
  • pH buffering — neutralizes acid from bacteria and diet
  • Pellicle formation — protein layer shields enamel surface

Lubrication & Mechanical Protection

  • Oral tissue lubrication (prevents friction injuries)
  • Prevention of mucosal abrasion
  • Upper GI tract lubrication

Antimicrobial & Immune Defense

  • Antibacterial activity (lysozyme, lactoferrin)
  • Antiviral and antifungal properties
  • Secretory IgA immunoglobulin secretion
  • Immune cell presence
  • Microbiome modulation

Digestion

  • Food bolus formation (chewing + swallowing)
  • Carbohydrate digestion (salivary amylase)
  • Lipid digestion (lingual lipase)
  • Esophageal acid neutralization

Sensory & Communication

  • Taste perception — saliva is the solvent for tastants (food molecules must dissolve in saliva to reach taste buds)
  • Taste sensitivity enhancement
  • Speech articulation — lubrication needed for tongue movement

Other Roles

  • Wound healing — growth factors in saliva (why oral wounds heal faster than skin)
  • Hormone synthesis
  • Diagnostic biomarker reservoir — saliva can be tested for drugs, hormones, antibodies, DNA
  • Cleansing & clearance — washes away food debris and bacteria

Stoopler ET, et al. 2024. JAMA. 331(12):1045–1054.

Major Salivary Gland Anatomy

Three paired major glands + hundreds of minor glands throughout the oral mucosa

There are two types of salivary secretion: serous (thin, watery, fast-flowing — like a waterfall) and mucous (thick, syrupy, slow-flowing — like honey). Each gland produces a different ratio of these, which determines the character of its saliva.

Gland Secretion Type Key Facts
Parotid Serous (purely watery) Largest gland. In front of ear/along jaw. Drains via Stensen duct opposite upper 2nd molar. Produces thin, enzyme-rich saliva.
Submandibular Mixed (serous + mucous) Below jaw. Drains via Wharton duct under tongue. Produces ~70% of resting saliva. Most prone to stones (sialolithiasis).
Sublingual Mucous (predominantly) Floor of mouth. Multiple small ducts (ducts of Rivinus). Produces thick, mucin-rich saliva.
Image: TBD — Anatomical illustration: parotid (serous), submandibular (mixed), sublingual (mucous) in lateral head view. Serous = waterfall; mucous = syrup analogy.

New Discovery: Tubarial Glands

In 2021, researchers discovered a 4th pair of major salivary glands — the tubarial glands — located in the nasopharynx near the Eustachian tube openings. These were identified using PSMA PET-CT scans and are clinically relevant because they may be damaged during radiation therapy for head and neck cancers, contributing to post-radiation dry mouth.

Valstar MH, et al. 2021. Radiother Oncol. 154:292–298.

Section 02

Hyposalivation & Xerostomia

These are NOT the same thing. This distinction is one of the most commonly tested concepts in oral medicine.

Image: TBD — Venn diagram: hyposalivation 40–60% vs xerostomia 20–42%, overlap 16–27%

Terminology: Hyposalivation vs. Xerostomia

One is measurable. The other is subjective. Only one causes dental damage.

Hyposalivation

Objective decrease in salivary output — this means you can measure it with a test.

  • Unstimulated salivary flow rate ≤0.1 mL/min
  • Stimulated salivary flow rate ≤0.5–0.7 mL/min
  • Prevalence: 40–60% of population

Xerostomia

Subjective sensation of dryness — a symptom, not a measurement. The patient feels dry.

  • Does NOT require an objective decrease in salivary flow
  • Can occur even with normal saliva production
  • Noted at 40–50% of normal salivary flow
  • Prevalence: 20–42% of population

Critical concept: Only hyposalivation increases dental and medical risk. Xerostomia alone (without measurably reduced saliva) does not cause caries, candidiasis, or other oral complications. But the patient still suffers from the sensation and deserves treatment for comfort.

Analogy: Hyposalivation is like your faucet actually running dry (you can measure zero water coming out). Xerostomia is like feeling thirsty even though your faucet works fine. Both are real complaints, but only the broken faucet causes actual water damage to your house (teeth).

The Overlap

These two conditions overlap 16–27% of the time. That means many patients have one without the other:

  • A patient can have hyposalivation WITHOUT xerostomia — their saliva is objectively low but they haven't noticed
  • A patient can have xerostomia WITHOUT hyposalivation — they feel dry but their flow rate is normal (may be due to medications, burning mouth syndrome, oral dysesthesia, or trigeminal neuropathy)

Morita I, et al. 2023. PLoS One. 18(3):e0282740. · Islas-Granillo H, et al. 2017. Sci Rep. 7:40686. · Diep MT, et al. 2021. Eur J Oral Sci. 129(1):e12757. · Rojas-Ramirez MV, et al. 2023. OSOMOPOR. 136(2):154–161.

Why Dry Mouths Have Thick, Ropey Saliva

Submandibular and sublingual glands are usually affected first in hyposalivation

When salivary output drops, you lose mostly water, not mucin (the protein that makes saliva thick). So the remaining saliva becomes concentrated with mucins — making it stringy, ropey, and sticky instead of thin and watery. The submandibular and sublingual glands (which produce most of the mucous component) are typically affected first, meaning the serous (watery) output from the parotid becomes the dominant remaining flow. But overall volume is so low that the thick, mucin-heavy residue is what you see clinically.

Three reasons why dry mouths look the way they do:

  • Water loss >> mucin loss — the watery component evaporates/decreases faster than the mucin proteins
  • Remaining mucins become concentrated in a smaller volume of fluid — like reducing a sauce, it gets thicker
  • Mucins become altered — saliva isn't as lubricating, is more stringy, and more sticky

Consequences of Hyposalivation

The clinical sequelae that make hyposalivation a dental emergency

Xerostomia alone does not cause these complications — hyposalivation does. This is a board-testable distinction. If a patient feels dry but has normal flow, they won't develop radiation-pattern caries.

  • Root/cervical caries — characteristic location pattern (detailed in Radiation section below)
  • Recurrent decay — loss of buffering + remineralization = rapid breakdown of previous restorations
  • Candidiasis — especially erythematous candidiasis (red, not white). Saliva normally suppresses fungal overgrowth via antimicrobial proteins and mechanical flushing
  • Dysphagia — difficulty swallowing because saliva is needed to form a food bolus and lubricate the esophagus
  • Mucosal trauma — dry tissue tears and ulcerates more easily
  • Difficulty wearing dentures — saliva creates the thin film that helps dentures adhere to tissue via suction

Stoopler ET, et al. 2024. JAMA. 331(12):1045–1054. · Plemons JM, et al. 2014. J Am Dent Assoc. 145(8):867–873.

Section 03

Chairside Saliva Assessment

How to evaluate a patient who reports dry mouth — a systematic three-step approach.

Image: TBD — Challacombe Scale clinical photographs (mild/moderate/severe dryness features)

The Three-Step Evaluation

Step 1: History

  • Medical history
    • Radiation history (head & neck?)
    • Autoimmune history (Sjögren, RA, SLE, thyroid disease?)
  • Medications — complete list, including OTC and supplements. Count total number (polypharmacy threshold: >3)
  • Radiation history — dose, field, when?
  • Symptoms
    • Duration — how long has this been happening?
    • Other body sites — dry eyes? dry nose? dry throat?
    • Difficulty swallowing — sips of water with meals? choking?

Step 2: Clinical Exam

Use the Challacombe Scale — a standardized scoring system with 10 clinical features to classify dryness severity:

Mild Score 1–3
Moderate Score 4–6
Severe Score 7–10
# Clinical Feature Severity
1Mirror sticks to buccal mucosaMild (1–3)
May not need treatment. Sugar-free gum for 15 min, twice daily. Attention to hydration. Routine checkup monitoring.
2Mirror sticks to tongue
3Saliva frothy
4No saliva pooling in floor of mouthModerate (4–6)
Needs investigation if causes unclear. Saliva substitutes, topical fluoride. Monitor for caries at regular intervals.
5Tongue shows generalized shortened papillae (mild depapillation)
6Altered gingival architecture (i.e., smooth)
7Glassy appearance of oral mucosa, especially palateSevere (7–10)
Cause must be ascertained. Rule out Sjögren syndrome. Refer for investigation & diagnosis. Saliva substitutes + topical fluoride. Monitor for changing symptoms with specialist input.
8Tongue lobulated / fissured
9Cervical caries (more than two teeth)
10Debris on palate or sticking to teeth

Osailan SM, et al. 2012. Oral Surg Oral Med Oral Pathol Oral Radiol. 114(5):597–603.

Step 3: Objective Testing (When Indicated)

  • Unstimulated whole saliva flow — patient drools into a graduated tube for 5–15 min. Normal: ≥0.1 mL/min
  • Stimulated flow — patient chews paraffin wax or uses citric acid, then collects saliva. Normal: ≥0.7 mL/min
  • Consider referral for additional testing if autoimmune etiology suspected
A 65-year-old patient has a concern of dry mouth. On exam, you see normal salivary pooling. What is your next step?
  • Prescribe pilocarpine immediately
  • Tell the patient everything is fine and no workup is needed
  • Measure salivary flow to distinguish xerostomia from hyposalivation
  • Refer to Rheumatology for Sjögren workup
Correct: Measure salivary flow. Normal pooling on exam suggests salivary output may be adequate — but the patient's subjective complaint is real. You need objective flow rate measurement to determine if this is true hyposalivation (which carries dental risk) or xerostomia without reduced flow (which may be neurological/medication-related and managed differently). Don't dismiss the patient, but don't jump to prescribing sialagogues or specialist referral without confirming the diagnosis first.
Section 04

Causes of Hyposalivation

Medications and radiation are the two biggest culprits. Know the risk tiers and the specific caries patterns.

Medication-Induced Hyposalivation

The most common cause of dry mouth in your patients

Very High Risk

  • Urological anticholinergics (bladder meds)
    • Oxybutynin OR 18.9
    • Tolterodine OR 4.8

High Risk

  • Antidepressants OR 4.7
  • Psycholeptics OR 2.6
  • Anticholinergics (all classes)
  • Antihypertensives
  • Antipsychotics
  • Sedatives

Board Pearl: Polypharmacy

Polypharmacy increases dry mouth risk independent of the specific drug. It's not just about which medications — it's about how many.

Use of >3 medications/day increases dry mouth risk with an odds ratio of OR 2.9

This means: even if none of the individual medications are "high risk" for dry mouth, combining 4+ drugs together creates additive anticholinergic burden that suppresses salivary output. Always count total meds.

Stoopler ET, et al. 2024. JAMA. 331(12):1045–1054. · Tan ECK, et al. 2017. J Am Geriatr Soc. 65(1):76–84.

Radiation-Induced Hyposalivation

Head & neck radiation causes permanent, dose-dependent gland destruction

Key Features

  • Standard head & neck radiation dose: 65–70 Gy
  • Dose-dependent gland destruction — higher dose = more damage, eventually irreversible
  • Tolerance thresholds for irreversible changes:
    • Parotid: >25 Gy
    • Submandibular: >39 Gy

Radiation caries has a characteristic pattern (this is highly testable):

  • Ipsilateral — on the same side as the radiation field
  • Cervical — at the neck of the tooth (CEJ), facial to circumferential
  • Smooth surfaces of mandibular anterior dentition — these surfaces are normally caries-resistant, so decay here is a red flag
  • Maxillary molars

Normally, the smooth labial surfaces of your lower front teeth never get cavities because saliva bathes them constantly from the sublingual glands right underneath. When radiation destroys those glands, these normally-protected surfaces become sitting ducks. That is why caries on the facial smooth surface of mandibular anteriors is a classic red flag for radiation-induced hyposalivation.

Image: TBD — Radiation caries: (A) cervical/root caries CEJ-level decay; (B) mandibular anterior smooth surface caries; (C) widespread cervical caries panoramic view.

Management of Radiation-Induced Hyposalivation

Aggressive Fluoride Protocol

  • Daily 1.1% sodium fluoride (NaF) gel or stannous fluoride (SnF2) gel
  • Applied via brush-on technique or custom dental trays
  • Daily 1.1% NaF dentifrice (5000 ppm fluoride) — e.g., PreviDent 5000
  • Fluoride varnish application 3× per year (alternative or adjunct)
  • Calcium phosphate artificial saliva rinse/cream/gel for remineralization

Recall & Caries Control

  • Short recall intervals: every 3–4 months
  • Brushing 2–4× daily
  • Chlorhexidine (CHX) rinses

Plemons JM, et al. 2014. J Am Dent Assoc. 145(8):867–873. · Mariette X, Criswell LA. 2018. N Engl J Med. 378(10):931–939.

Section 05

Treatment of Hyposalivation & Xerostomia

A tiered approach: things to avoid, OTC options, and prescription sialagogues with their pharmacology.

Image: TBD — Sialagogue mechanism: muscarinic receptor (M3) activation pathway diagram

Non-Pharmacologic Management

Avoid

  • Caffeine — mild diuretic, can worsen dehydration
  • Alcohol — both beverages and alcohol-containing mouthwash (e.g., original Listerine). Alcohol is drying.
  • Vaseline / Aquaphor on lips — petroleum-based products seal moisture OUT, not in. Use lanolin instead.

Over-the-Counter Options

  • Hydration (+/- pineapple — bromelain may stimulate saliva)
  • Bedside humidifier — especially for nighttime mouth breathing
  • Sugar-free lemon candies — citric acid stimulates residual gland function
  • XyliMelts — xylitol adhesive discs that stick to gums/teeth
  • Xerostrips — dissolving oral strips
  • Biotene products — rinse, spray, gel, toothpaste (enzyme-based saliva substitutes)
  • Mouth Kote — moisturizing spray
  • Lanolin HPA (Lansinoh) — for dry/cracked lips. Better than petroleum because it lets moisture pass through.

Pharmacologic Treatment: Sialagogues

Prescription drugs that stimulate salivary glands to produce more saliva by activating muscarinic receptors

What are muscarinic receptors? They are a type of receptor for the neurotransmitter acetylcholine, found throughout the body. There are 5 subtypes (M1–M5). The one we care most about is M3, which is the receptor on salivary glands, lacrimal glands, sweat glands, and smooth muscle. When M3 is activated, it tells the gland to secrete. Sialagogues are drugs that activate M3 to make salivary glands produce more saliva.

Sialagogue contraindications (because muscarinic activation affects more than just salivary glands): Asthma (M3 causes bronchoconstriction), narrow-angle glaucoma (M3 increases intraocular pressure), cardiac disease (M2 slows heart rate).

The Three Sialagogues

Drug Dose Receptor Selectivity Key Detail
Pilocarpine 5 mg TID–6x/day Non-selective (hits all M1–M5) but highest affinity for M3 Most commonly prescribed sialagogue. More side effects because it is non-selective (sweating, GI upset, flushing).
Cevimeline 30 mg TID Selective for M1 and M3 More targeted = fewer side effects than pilocarpine. Does not hit M2 (heart) as much.
Bethanechol 25 mg TID Selective for M2 and M3 Less commonly used for dry mouth. Hits M2 (cardiac effects).

Topical Alternative

2 drops of pilocarpine 4% ophthalmic solution in 1 teaspoon of water, hold in mouth for 5 minutes, then spit out. This provides local stimulation with fewer systemic side effects.

Muscarinic Receptor Reference Table

You don't need to memorize every detail of this table, but understand the pattern: M3 is the "secretion + smooth muscle" receptor. When sialagogues activate M3, the salivary glands secrete — but so do sweat glands (sweating side effect), bronchial smooth muscle contracts (why it's contraindicated in asthma), and GI motility increases (diarrhea side effect).

Receptor Primary Locations Normal Physiologic Effect
M1 CNS (cortex, hippocampus), autonomic ganglia, gastric parietal cells Cognitive modulation, facilitates autonomic transmission, ↑ gastric acid secretion
M2 Heart (SA node, AV node, atria) ↓ Heart rate, ↓ conduction velocity
M3 Salivary glands, lacrimal glands, sweat glands, bronchial smooth muscle, GI smooth muscle, bladder detrusor muscle, vascular endothelium ↑ Exocrine secretions (saliva, tears, sweat), bronchoconstriction, ↑ GI motility, bladder contraction, endothelial NO release (vasodilation)
M4 CNS (less well defined clinically) Modulates dopamine pathways
M5 CNS, cerebral vasculature Cerebral vasodilation (via NO)

Stoopler ET, et al. 2024. JAMA. 331(12):1045–1054. · Plemons JM, et al. 2014. J Am Dent Assoc. 145(8):867–873.

Section 06

Sjögren Syndrome

Autoimmune destruction of salivary and lacrimal glands. The quintessential "dry mouth + dry eyes" disease with a dangerous complication: lymphoma.

Image: TBD — Bilateral parotid swelling in Sjögren patient; oral purpura; labial salivary gland biopsy (focal lymphocytic sialadenitis)

Sjögren Overview

Sjögren syndrome is an autoimmune disease where your immune system attacks and destroys your own moisture-producing glands — primarily salivary and lacrimal (tear) glands. Think of it as your body's immune system systematically dismantling its own plumbing. The result: dry mouth, dry eyes, and a significantly elevated risk of lymphoma.

Definition & Classification

Autoimmune destruction of salivary and lacrimal glands

Primary Sjögren Syndrome

Occurs alone — no other autoimmune disease present

Secondary Sjögren Syndrome

Occurs alongside another autoimmune disease (most commonly RA or SLE)

Epidemiology

  • Middle-aged females
  • Female:male ratio = 9:1
  • Prevalence: 0.5–1% of population

Classic Presentation

  • Hyposalivation +/- xerostomia
  • Xerophthalmia (dry eyes)
  • Parotid enlargement (bilateral)
  • Middle-aged female
  • Autoimmune comorbidity (RA, thyroid disease, etc.)

Must Exclude Before Diagnosing

  • History of head/neck radiation
  • Active hepatitis C infection
  • Sarcoidosis
  • IgG4-related disease
  • Recent anticholinergic use (can cause false positive findings)

Zero DT, et al. 2016. J Am Dent Assoc. 147(4):295–305. · Goldblatt F, O'Neill SG. 2013. Lancet. 382(9894):797–808.

Diagnostic Workup: 2016 ACR-EULAR Criteria

Current classification requires a score ≥4 points

Know this scoring system for the exam. The two "3-point" items (biopsy and anti-SSA) are the heavy hitters. Either one alone gets you 75% of the way to diagnosis.

Criterion Points Details
Labial salivary gland biopsy with focal lymphocytic sialadenitis (focus score ≥1) 3 points Also correlates with increased lymphoma risk
Anti-SSA (Ro) positive 3 points The most important serological marker
Ocular staining score ≥5 (or Schirmer test ≤5 mm/5 min) 1 point Measures tear production / corneal damage
Unstimulated whole saliva flow ≤0.1 mL/min 1 point Objective hyposalivation
Anti-SSB (La) positive 1 point Less specific than anti-SSA; often co-occurs

What to do as a dentist when you suspect Sjögren:

  • Order Anti-SSA (Ro) and Anti-SSB (La) antibodies
  • Also order ANA (antinuclear antibody) and RF (rheumatoid factor)
  • Refer to Oral Medicine or OMFS for minor salivary gland biopsy

Early detection matters: preserving gland function early (DMARDs, clinical trials) is better than managing the consequences of complete gland destruction later.

Zero DT, et al. 2016. J Am Dent Assoc. 147(4):295–305. · Mariette X, Criswell LA. 2018. N Engl J Med. 378(10):931–939. · Goldblatt F, O'Neill SG. 2013. Lancet. 382(9894):797–808.

A 52-year-old woman with objectively dry mouth and dry eyes has negative anti-SSA and anti-SSB. Can she have Sjögren syndrome?
  • Yes — up to 30% of patients with positive biopsy/Schirmer may be seronegative for antibodies
  • No — anti-SSA is required for the diagnosis
  • No — if both antibodies are negative, Sjögren is definitively ruled out
  • Only if she has rheumatoid arthritis
Correct: Yes — seronegative Sjögren exists. Up to 30% of patients may have positive biopsy and/or Schirmer test findings despite negative serology. A negative antibody result does not rule out the disease. Management: increase recall frequency, start high-potency fluoride therapy, counsel on caries risk and OHI, refer to Oral Medicine (for minor salivary gland biopsy, ocular staining or Schirmer test) and Rheumatology, consider sialagogue therapy, OTC saliva products, and encourage hydration.

Lymphoma Risk in Sjögren Syndrome

The most dangerous complication — and the reason we monitor these patients closely

Critical complication:

  • Lifetime risk of non-Hodgkin lymphoma: 5–10%
  • Specifically: Mucosa-Associated Lymphoid Tissue (MALT) lymphoma
  • Highest risk of lymphoma among all autoimmune diseases
  • 7–19× higher risk than general population

Red Flags Requiring Urgent Rheumatology/Hematology Referral

  • Persistent unilateral gland enlargement — bilateral is typical Sjögren; unilateral suggests neoplasm
  • Lymphadenopathy
  • Purpura (purple skin spots from bleeding into skin)
  • Constitutional symptoms — fever, night sweats, weight loss (the classic "B symptoms" of lymphoma)

Key distinction: Bilateral parotid enlargement = typical Sjögren (autoimmune inflammation). Unilateral persistent enlargement = worry about lymphoma or other neoplasm. This is a critical exam distinction.

Image: TBD — Lymphoma warning signs: bilateral parotid swelling, intraoral purpura on palate, oral MALT lymphoma lesions, sublingual varices/mucosal changes.
Section 07

Obstructive & Infectious Conditions

Salivary stones and bacterial infections — mechanical blockages and their consequences.

Image: TBD — Sialolithiasis imaging: panoramic radiograph showing submandibular sialolith; occlusal film floor of mouth stone

Sialolithiasis (Salivary Stones)

Calcified masses that block salivary ducts, causing pain and swelling during meals

Classic Pattern

Pathognomonic finding: meal-time pain and swelling. The gland swells because it is producing saliva in response to eating but the stone blocks the duct, so the saliva backs up. The swelling goes down between meals when the gland stops producing actively.

  • Submandibular gland is most common — 80–90% of all salivary stones
  • Unilateral presentation (one side)

Why Is the Submandibular Gland Most Commonly Affected?

Three anatomical reasons conspire against the submandibular gland:

  • Longer, more tortuous duct — Wharton's duct is longer and has more twists than Stensen's duct (parotid), creating more opportunity for stones to form and get stuck
  • Saliva flows against gravity — the gland is below the floor of the mouth and the duct opening is above, so saliva must travel uphill. Stagnation promotes stone formation.
  • More mucous, calcium-rich saliva — the submandibular gland produces mixed (serous + mucous) saliva. The mucous component is thicker and contains more calcium, which precipitates into stones.

Imaging Options

Modality When to Use
Occlusal radiograph First-line for submandibular stones (can often see radiopaque stones in floor of mouth)
Panoramic radiograph Good overview; may show large stones
Ultrasound (via referral) Excellent for non-radiopaque stones that don't show up on X-rays
CT If needed for surgical planning

Not all stones are radiopaque — if your X-ray is negative but you still suspect a stone (classic meal-time symptoms), order ultrasound or CT. A negative radiograph does not rule out sialolithiasis.

Image: TBD — Panoramic radiograph: edentulous patient with radiopaque sialolith in right submandibular region.

Management

Conservative (First-Line)

  • Hydration (2L/day)
  • Gland massage — milk the gland toward the duct opening
  • Warm/moist heat compresses
  • OTC saliva products
  • NSAIDs or acetaminophen for pain
  • Muscarinic receptor agonist therapy (sialagogues — increase flow to flush the stone)

When to Refer for Sialendoscopy

  • Stone >2 mm in proximal duct
  • Failed conservative management after 1–2 weeks
  • Recurrent symptoms
  • Success rate: 85–92% gland preservation

Large / Recurrent Stones

  • Refer for sialendoscopy
  • Surgical removal if needed
Image: TBD — Sialendoscopy: endoscope insertion into Wharton's duct; extracorporeal shockwave lithotripsy; endoscopic duct lumen view; retrieved stone via wire basket.

National Comprehensive Cancer Network. 2025. Head and Neck Cancers. · Kim MJ, et al. 2024. Am Fam Physician. 109(3):257–266. · Nahlieli O. 2025. Br J Oral Maxillofac Surg. 63(1):34–41.

Acute Bacterial Sialadenitis

Bacterial infection of a salivary gland — typically secondary to obstruction or dehydration

When saliva stagnates (from a stone, dehydration, or post-surgical state), bacteria can ascend the duct and infect the gland. The classic scenario: a debilitated, dehydrated post-surgical patient develops a painful, swollen parotid gland. Or a patient with a known stone develops secondary infection.

Clinical Signs

  • Painful swollen gland (unilateral)
  • Fever
  • Purulence at duct orifice — if you milk the gland and pus comes out of the duct opening, that is diagnostic
  • Erythema (redness of overlying skin)
  • Firm, tender gland on palpation

Common Organisms

  • Staphylococcus aureus — most common
  • Streptococcus species
  • Anaerobes
Image: TBD — Acute sialadenitis: massive unilateral parotid/submandibular swelling with visible skin erythema and tension.

Management

Treatment

  • Antibiotics
    • First-line: Amoxicillin-clavulanate or Clindamycin
    • Must cover Staph aureus
  • Aggressive hydration
  • Culture if purulent discharge present
  • Imaging to rule out obstruction (stone? mass?)

Urgent Referral (ED/ENT/OMFS) If:

  • Systemic spread / sepsis
  • Immunocompromised patient
  • Trismus / severe swelling
  • Airway compromise concern

National Comprehensive Cancer Network. 2025. Head and Neck Cancers (Version 2025). · Kim MJ, et al. 2024. Am Fam Physician. 109(3):257–266.

Section 08

Mucocele

A common benign lesion caused by trauma to minor salivary gland ducts. Know where they occur and why.

Image: TBD — Mucocele on lower lip: dome-shaped, bluish, fluctuant. Surgical excision sequence.

Mucocele

Mucus retention/extravasation phenomenon of minor salivary glands

Classic Presentation

  • Lower lip — most common site: 60–70% of cases
  • Fluctuant swelling — soft, compressible, dome-shaped
  • Bluish color — mucus showing through the thin overlying mucosa
  • History of trauma — lip biting is the classic cause (sharp canine teeth biting the lower lip)
  • Painless
  • May rupture and reform cyclically

Pathophysiology

You bite your lip, and the force severs a tiny minor salivary gland duct. The duct keeps trying to pump mucus, but now it has nowhere to go. The mucus spills out into the surrounding connective tissue, forming a pool surrounded by inflamed tissue (granulation wall). This is NOT a true cyst — it has no epithelial lining. It is technically a "mucus extravasation phenomenon."

Extravasation Type (Most Common)

  • Trauma severs the duct
  • Mucus spills into connective tissue
  • Surrounded by granulation wall (inflammatory reaction)
  • No epithelial lining — therefore NOT a true cyst
  • Common in younger patients

Retention Type (Less Common)

  • Duct becomes partially blocked (not severed)
  • Mucus backs up within the duct itself
  • Duct dilates and forms a mucus-filled sac
  • HAS an epithelial lining (the duct wall) — IS a true cyst
  • More common in older adults
Image: TBD — Mucocele excision 4-panel: dome-shaped lesion → excision with forceps → excised specimen → post-excision wound bed on inner lower lip.
Image: TBD — Diagram: Extravasation type (severed duct, mucin pooling freely, granulation wall, no epithelial lining) vs. Retention type (obstructed intact duct, dilated with mucin, epithelial duct wall maintained).

Why lower lip? The lower lip is the most commonly traumatized mucosal surface in the mouth. It sits right over the lower incisor/canine teeth, which act as a biting surface during accidental lip biting, habitual chewing, or trauma. The lower lip is also packed with minor salivary glands whose tiny ducts are vulnerable to rupture.

Treatment

  • Surgical excision — remove the mucocele along with the associated minor salivary gland to prevent recurrence
  • Must remove the feeding gland, not just drain the mucus — otherwise it will refill
  • If the mucocele is very large (on the floor of the mouth) it is called a ranula — these may require marsupialization or removal of the sublingual gland
